JSONSerializer template argument\n902|\n903|This serializer ignores the template arguments and uses ADL\n904|([argument-dependent lookup](http://en.cppreference.com/w/cpp/language/adl))\n905|for serialization.\n906|*/\n907|template<typename = void, typename = void>\n908|struct adl_serializer\n909|{\n910|    /*!\n911|    @brief convert a JSON value to any value type\n912|\n913|    This function is usually called by the `get()` function of the\n914|    @ref basic_json class (either explicit or via conversion operators).\n915|\n916|    @param[in] j         JSON value to read   /*!\n927|    @brief convert any value type to a JSON value\n928|\n929|    This function is usually called by the constructors of the @ref basic_json\n930|    class.\n931|\n932|    @param[in,out] j }\n941|};\n942|\n943|\n944|/*!\n945|@brief a class to store JSON values\n946|\n947|@tparam ObjectType type for JSON objects (`std::map` by default; will be used\n948|in @ref object_t)\n949|@tparam ArrayType type for JSON arrays (`std::vector` by default; will be used\n950|in @ref array_t)\n951|@tparam StringType type for JSON strings and object keys (`std::string` by\n952|default; will be used in @ref string_t)\n953|@tparam BooleanType type for JSON booleans (`bool` by default; will be used\n954|in @ref boolean_t)\n955|@tparam NumberIntegerType type for JSON integer numbers (`int64_t` by\n956|default; will be used in @ref number_integer_t)\n957|@tparam NumberUnsignedType type for JSON unsigned integer numbers (@c\n958|`uint64_t` by default; will be used in @ref number_unsigned_t)\n959|@tparam NumberFloatType type for JSON floating-point numbers (`double` by\n960|default; will be used in @ref number_float_t)\n961|@tparam AllocatorType type of the allocator to use (`std::allocator` by\n962|default)\n963|@tparam JSONSerializer the serializer to resolve internal calls to `to_json()`\n964|and `from_json()` (@ref adl_serializer by default)\n965|\n966|@requirement The class satisfies the following concept requirements:\n967|- Basic\n968| - [DefaultConstructible](http://en.cppreference.com/w/cpp/concept/DefaultConstructible):\n969|   JSON values can be default constructed. The result will be a JSON null\n970|   value.\n971| - [MoveConstructible](http://en.cppreference.com/w/cpp/concept/MoveConstructible):\n972|   A JSON value can be constructed from an rvalue argument.\n973| - [CopyConstructible](http://en.cppreference.com/w/cpp/concept/CopyConstructible):\n974|   A JSON value can be copy-constructed from an lvalue expression.\n975| - [MoveAssignable](http://en.cppreference.com/w/cpp/concept/MoveAssignable):\n976|   A JSON value van be assigned from an rvalue argument.\n977| - [CopyAssignable](http://en.cppreference.com/w/cpp/concept/CopyAssignable):\n978|   A JSON value can be copy-assigned from an lvalue expression.\n979| - [Destructible](http://en.cppreference.com/w/cpp/concept/Destructible):\n980|   JSON values can be destructed.\n981|- Layout\n982| - [StandardLayoutType](http://en.cppreference.com/w/cpp/concept/StandardLayoutType):\n983|   JSON values have\n984|   [standard layout](http://en.cppreference.com/w/cpp/language/data_members#Standard_layout):\n985|   All non-static data members are private and standard layout types, the\n986|   class has no virtual functions or (virtual) base classes.\n987|- Library-wide\n988| - [EqualityComparable](http://en.cppreference.com/w/cpp/concept/EqualityComparable):\n989|   JSON values can be compared with `==`, see @ref\n990|   operator==(const_reference,const_reference).\n991| - [LessThanComparable](http://en.cppreference.com/w/cpp/concept/LessThanComparable):\n992|   JSON values can be compared with `<`, see @ref\n993|   operator<(const_reference,const_reference).\n994| - [Swappable](http://en.cppreference.com/w/cpp/concept/Swappable):\n995|   Any JSON lvalue or rvalue of can be swapped with any lvalue or rvalue of\n996|   other compatible types, using unqualified function call @ref swap().\n997| - [NullablePointer](http://en.cppreference.com/w/cpp/concept/NullablePointer):\n998|   JSON values can be compared against `std::nullptr_t` objects which are used\n999|   to model the `null` value.\n1000|- Container\n1001| - [Container](http://en.cppreference.com/w/cpp/concept/Container):\n1002|   JSON values can be used like STL containers and provide iterator access.\n1003| - [ReversibleContainer](http://en.cppreference.com/w/cpp/concept/ReversibleContainer);\n1004|   JSON values can be used like STL containers and provide reverse iterator\n1005|   access.\n1006|\n1007|@invariant The member variables @a m_value and @a m_type have the following\n1008|relationship:\n1009|- If `m_type == value_t::object`, then `m_value.object != nullptr`.\n1010|- If `m_type == value_t::array`, then `m_value.array != nullptr`.\n1011|- If `m_type == value_t::string`, then `m_value.string != nullptr`.\n1012|The invariants are checked by member function assert_invariant().\n1013|\n1014|@internal\n1015|@note ObjectType trick from http://stackoverflow.com/a/9860911\n1016|@endinternal\n1017|\n1018|@see [RFC 7159: The JavaScript Object Notation (JSON) Data Interchange\n1019|Format](http://rfc7159.net/rfc7159)\n1020|\n1021|@since version 1.0.0\n1022|\n1023|@nosubgrouping\n1024|*/\n1025|template <\n1026|    template<typename U, typename V, typename... basic_json container\n1087|    using const_iterator = iter_impl<const basic_json>;\n1088|    /// a reverse iterator for a basic_json container\n1089|    using reverse_iterator = json_reverse_iterator<typename basic_json::iterator>;\n1090|    /// a const reverse iterator for a basic_json container\n1091|    using const_reverse_iterator = json_reverse_iterator<typename basic_json::const_iterator>;\n1092|\n1093|    /// @}\n1094|\n1095|\n1096|    /*!\n1097|    @brief returns the allocator associated with the container\n1098|    */\n1099|    static allocator_type get_allocator()\n1100|    {\n1101|        return allocator_type();\n1102|    }\n1103|\n1104|    /*!\n1105|    @brief returns version information on the library\n1106|\n1107|    This function returns a JSON object with information about the library,\n1108|    including the version number and information on the platform and compiler.\n1109|\n1110|    @return JSON object holding version information\n1111|    key         | description\n1112|    ----------- | ---------------\n1113|    `compiler`  | Information on the used compiler. It is an object with the following keys: `c++` (the used C++ standard), `family` (the compiler family; possible values are `clang`, `icc`, `gcc`, `ilecpp`, `msvc`, `pgcpp`, `sunpro`, and `unknown`), and `version` (the compiler version).\n1114|    `copyright` | The copyright line for the library as string.\n1115|    `name`      | The name of the library as string.\n1116|    `platform`  | The used platform as string. Possible values are `win32`, `linux`, `apple`, `unix`, and `unknown`.\n1117|    `url`       | The URL of the project as string.\n1118|    `version`   | The version of the library. It is an object with the following keys: `major`, `minor`, and `patch` as defined by [Semantic Versioning](http://semver.org), and `string` (the version string).\n1119|\n1120|    @liveexample{The ///////////////////////////\n1181|    // JSON value data types //\n1182|    ///////////////////////////\n1183|\n1184|    /// @name JSON value data types\n1185|    /// The data types to store a JSON value. These types are derived from\n1186|    /// the template arguments passed to class @ref basic_json.\n1187|    /// @{\n1188|\n1189|    /*!\n1190|    @brief a type for an object\n1191|\n1192|    [RFC 7159](http://rfc7159.net/rfc7159) describes JSON objects as follows:\n1193|    > An object is an unordered collection of zero or more name/value pairs,\n1194|    > where a name is a string and a value is a string, number, boolean, null,\n1195|    > object, or array.\n1196|\n1197|    To store objects in C++, a type is defined by the template parameters\n1198|    described below.\n1199|\n1200|    @tparam ObjectType  the container to store objects (e.g., `std::map` or\n1201|    `std::unordered_map`)\n1202|    @tparam StringType the type of the keys or names (e.g., `std::string`).\n1203|    The comparison function `std::less<StringType>` is used to order elements\n1204|    inside the container.\n1205|    @tparam AllocatorType the allocator to use for objects (e.g.,\n1206|    `std::allocator`)\n1207|\n1208|    #### Default type\n1209|\n1210|    With the default values for @a ObjectType (`std::map`), @a StringType\n1211|    (`std::string`), and @a AllocatorType (`std::allocator`), the default\n1212|    value for @a object_t is:\n1213|\n1214|    @code {.cpp}\n1215|    std::map<\n1216|      std::string, // key_type\n1217|      basic_json, // value_type\n1218|      std::less<std::string>, // key_compare\n1219|      std::allocator<std::pair<const std::string, basic_json>> // allocator_type\n1220|    >\n1221|    @endcode\n1222|\n1223|    #### Behavior\n1224|\n1225|    The choice of @a object_t influences the behavior of the JSON class. With\n1226|    the default type, objects have the following behavior:\n1227|\n1228|    - When all names are unique, objects will be interoperable in the sense\n1229|      that all software implementations receiving that object will agree on\n1230|      the name-value mappings.\n1231|    - When the names within an object are not unique, later stored name/value\n1232|      pairs overwrite previously stored name/value pairs, leaving the used\n1233|      names unique. For instance, `{\"key\": 1}` and `{\"key\": 2, \"key\": 1}` will\n1234|      be treated as equal and both stored as `{\"key\": 1}`.\n1235|    - Internally, name/value pairs are stored in lexicographical order of the\n1236|      names. Objects will also be serialized (see @ref dump) in this order.\n1237|      For instance, `{\"b\": 1, \"a\": 2}` and `{\"a\": 2, \"b\": 1}` will be stored\n1238|      and serialized as `{\"a\": 2, \"b\": 1}`.\n1239|    - When comparing objects, the order of the name/value pairs is irrelevant.\n1240|      This makes objects interoperable in the sense that they will not be\n1241|      affected by these differences. For instance, `{\"b\": 1, \"a\": 2}` and\n1242|      `{\"a\": 2, \"b\": 1}` will be treated as equal.\n1243|\n1244|    #### Limits\n1245|\n1246|    [RFC 7159](http://rfc7159.net/rfc7159) specifies:\n1247|    > An implementation may set limits on the maximum depth of nesting.\n1248|\n1249|    In this class, the object's limit of nesting is not constraint explicitly.\n1250|    However, a maximum depth of nesting may be introduced by the compiler or\n1251|    runtime environment. A theoretical limit can be queried by calling the\n1252|    @ref max_size function of a JSON object.\n1253|\n1254|    #### Storage\n1255|\n1256|    Objects are stored as pointers in a @ref basic_json type. That is, for any\n1257|    access to object values, a pointer of type `object_t*` must be\n1258|    dereferenced.\n1259|\n1260|    @sa @ref array_t -- type for an array value\n1261|\n1262|    @since version 1.0.0\n1263|\n1264|    @note The order name/value pairs are added to the object is *not*\n1265|    preserved by the library. Therefore, iterating an object may return\n1266|    name/value pairs in a different order than they were originally stored. In\n1267|    fact, keys will be traversed in alphabetical order as `std::map` with\n1268|    `std::less` is used by default. Please note this behavior conforms to [RFC\n1269|    7159](http://rfc7159.net/rfc7159), because any order implements the\n1270|    specified \"unordered\" nature of JSON objects.\n1271|    */\n1272|    using object_t = ObjectType<StringType,\n1273|          basic_json,\n1274|          std::less<StringType>,\n1275|          AllocatorType<std::pair<const StringType,\n1276|          basic_json>>>;\n1277|\n1278|    /*!\n1279|    @brief a type for an array\n1280|\n1281|    [RFC 7159](http://rfc7159.net/rfc7159) describes JSON arrays as follows:\n1282|    > An array is an ordered sequence of zero or more values.\n1283|\n1284|    To store objects in C++, a type is defined by the template parameters\n1285|    explained below.\n1286|\n1287|    @tparam ArrayType  container type to store arrays (e.g., `std::vector` or\n1288|    `std::list`)\n1289|    @tparam AllocatorType allocator to use for arrays (e.g., `std::allocator`)\n1290|\n1291|    #### Default type\n1292|\n1293|    With the default values for @a ArrayType (`std::vector`) and @a\n1294|    AllocatorType (`std::allocator`), the default value for @a array_t is:\n1295|\n1296|    @code {.cpp}\n1297|    std::vector<\n1298|      basic_json, // value_type\n1299|      std::allocator<basic_json> // allocator_type\n1300|    >\n1301|    @endcode\n1302|\n1303|    #### Limits\n1304|\n1305|    [RFC 7159](http://rfc7159.net/rfc7159) specifies:\n1306|    > An implementation may set limits on the maximum depth of nesting.\n1307|\n1308|    In this class, the array's limit of nesting is not constraint explicitly.\n1309|    However, a maximum depth of nesting may be introduced by the compiler or\n1310|    runtime environment. A theoretical limit can be queried by calling the\n1311|    @ref max_size function of a JSON array.\n1312|\n1313|    #### Storage\n1314|\n1315|    Arrays are stored as pointers in a @ref basic_json type. That is, for any\n1316|    access to array values, a pointer of type `array_t*` must be dereferenced.\n1317|\n1318|    @sa @ref object_t -- type for an object value\n1319|\n1320|    @since version 1.0.0\n1321|    */\n1322|    using array_t = ArrayType<basic_json, AllocatorType<basic_json>>;\n1323|\n1324|    /*!\n1325|    @brief a type for a string\n1326|\n1327|    [RFC 7159](http://rfc7159.net/rfc7159) describes JSON strings as follows:\n1328|    > A string is a sequence of zero or more Unicode characters.\n1329|\n1330|    To store objects in C++, a type is defined by the template parameter\n1331|    described below. Unicode values are split by the JSON class into\n1332|    byte-sized characters during deserialization.\n1333|\n1334|    @tparam StringType  the container to store strings (e.g., `std::string`).\n1335|    Note this container is used for keys/names in objects, see @ref object_t.\n1336|\n1337|    #### Default type\n1338|\n1339|    With the default values for @a StringType (`std::string`), the default\n1340|    value for @a string_t is:\n1341|\n1342|    @code {.cpp}\n1343|    std::string\n1344|    @endcode\n1345|\n1346|    #### Encoding\n1347|\n1348|    Strings are stored in UTF-8 encoding. Therefore, functions like\n1349|    `std::string::size()` or `std::string::length()` return the number of\n1350|    bytes in the string rather than the number of characters or glyphs.\n1351|\n1352|    #### String comparison\n1353|\n1354|    [RFC 7159](http://rfc7159.net/rfc7159) states:\n1355|    > Software implementations are typically required to test names of object\n1356|    > members for equality. Implementations that transform the textual\n1357|    > representation into sequences of Unicode code units and then perform the\n1358|    > comparison numerically, code unit by code unit, are interoperable in the\n1359|    > sense that implementations will agree in all cases on equality or\n1360|    > inequality of two strings. For example, implementations that compare\n1361|    > strings with escaped characters unconverted may incorrectly find that\n1362|    > `\"a\\\\b\"` and `\"a\\u005Cb\"` are not equal.\n1363|\n1364|    This implementation is interoperable as it does compare strings code unit\n1365|    by code unit.\n1366|\n1367|    #### Storage\n1368|\n1369|    String values are stored as pointers in a @ref basic_json type. That is,\n1370|    for any access to string values, a pointer of type `string_t*` must be\n1371|    dereferenced.\n1372|\n1373|    @since version 1.0.0\n1374|    */\n1375|    using string_t = StringType;\n1376|\n1377|    /*!\n1378|    @brief a type for a boolean\n1379|\n1380|    [RFC 7159](http://rfc7159.net/rfc7159) implicitly describes a boolean as a\n1381|    type which differentiates the two literals `true` and `false`.\n1382|\n1383|    To store objects in C++, a type is defined by the template parameter @a\n1384|    BooleanType which chooses the type to use.\n1385|\n1386|    #### Default type\n1387|\n1388|    With the default values for @a BooleanType (`bool`), the default value for\n1389|    @a boolean_t is:\n1390|\n1391|    @code {.cpp}\n1392|    bool\n1393|    @endcode\n1394|\n1395|    #### Storage\n1396|\n1397|    Boolean values are stored directly inside a @ref basic_json type.\n1398|\n1399|    @since version 1.0.0\n1400|    */\n1401|    using boolean_t = BooleanType;\n1402|\n1403|    /*!\n1404|    @brief a type for a number (integer)\n1405|\n1406|    [RFC 7159](http://rfc7159.net/rfc7159) describes numbers as follows:\n1407|    > The representation of numbers is similar to that used in most\n1408|    > programming languages. A number is represented in base 10 using decimal\n1409|    > digits. It contains an integer component that may be prefixed with an\n1410|    > optional minus sign, which may be followed by a fraction part and/or an\n1411|    > exponent part. Leading zeros are not allowed. (...) Numeric values that\n1412|    > cannot be represented in the grammar below (such as Infinity and NaN)\n1413|    > are not permitted.\n1414|\n1415|    This description includes both integer and floating-point numbers.\n1416|    However, C++ allows more precise storage if it is known whether the number\n1417|    is a signed integer, an unsigned integer or a floating-point number.\n1418|    Therefore, three different types, @ref number_integer_t, @ref\n1419|    number_unsigned_t and @ref number_float_t are used.\n1420|\n1421|    To store integer numbers in C++, a type is defined by the template\n1422|    parameter @a NumberIntegerType which chooses the type to use.\n1423|\n1424|    #### Default type\n1425|\n1426|    With the default values for @a NumberIntegerType (`int64_t`), the default\n1427|    value for @a number_integer_t is:\n1428|\n1429|    @code {.cpp}\n1430|    int64_t\n1431|    @endcode\n1432|\n1433|    #### Default behavior\n1434|\n1435|    - The restrictions about leading zeros is not enforced in C++. Instead,\n1436|      leading zeros in integer literals lead to an interpretation as octal\n1437|      number. Internally, the value will be stored as decimal number. For\n1438|      instance, the C++ integer literal `010` will be serialized to `8`.\n1439|      During deserialization, leading zeros yield an error.\n1440|    - Not-a-number (NaN) values will be serialized to `null`.\n1441|\n1442|    #### Limits\n1443|\n1444|    [RFC 7159](http://rfc7159.net/rfc7159) specifies:\n1445|    > An implementation may set limits on the range and precision of numbers.\n1446|\n1447|    When the default type is used, the maximal integer number that can be\n1448|    stored is `9223372036854775807` (INT64_MAX) and the minimal integer number\n1449|    that can be stored is `-9223372036854775808` (INT64_MIN). Integer numbers\n1450|    that are out of range will yield over/underflow when used in a\n1451|    constructor. During deserialization, too large or small integer numbers\n1452|    will be automatically be stored as @ref number_unsigned_t or @ref\n1453|    number_float_t.\n1454|\n1455|    [RFC 7159](http://rfc7159.net/rfc7159) further states:\n1456|    > Note that when such software is used, numbers that are integers and are\n1457|    > in the range \\f$[-2^{53}+1, 2^{53}-1]\\f$ are interoperable in the sense\n1458|    > that implementations will agree exactly on their numeric values.\n1459|\n1460|    As this range is a subrange of the exactly supported range [INT64_MIN,\n1461|    INT64_MAX], this class's integer type is interoperable.\n1462|\n1463|    #### Storage\n1464|\n1465|    Integer number values are stored directly inside a @ref basic_json type.\n1466|\n1467|    @sa @ref number_float_t -- type for number values (floating-point)\n1468|\n1469|    @sa @ref number_unsigned_t -- type for number values (unsigned integer)\n1470|\n1471|    @since version 1.0.0\n1472|    */\n1473|    using number_integer_t = NumberIntegerType;\n1474|\n1475|    /*!\n1476|    @brief a type for a number (unsigned)\n1477|\n1478|    [RFC 7159](http://rfc7159.net/rfc7159) describes numbers as follows:\n1479|    > The representation of numbers is similar to that used in most\n1480|    > programming languages. A number is represented in base 10 using decimal\n1481|    > digits. It contains an integer component that may be prefixed with an\n1482|    > optional minus sign, which may be followed by a fraction part and/or an\n1483|    > exponent part. Leading zeros are not allowed. (...) Numeric values that\n1484|    > cannot be represented in the grammar below (such as Infinity and NaN)\n1485|    > are not permitted.\n1486|\n1487|    This description includes both integer and floating-point numbers.\n1488|    However, C++ allows more precise storage if it is known whether the number\n1489|    is a signed integer, an unsigned integer or a floating-point number.\n1490|    Therefore, three different types, @ref number_integer_t, @ref\n1491|    number_unsigned_t and @ref number_float_t are used.\n1492|\n1493|    To store unsigned integer numbers in C++, a type is defined by the\n1494|    template parameter @a NumberUnsignedType which chooses the type to use.\n1495|\n1496|    #### Default type\n1497|\n1498|    With the default values for @a NumberUnsignedType (`uint64_t`), the\n1499|    default value for @a number_unsigned_t is:\n1500|\n1501|    @code {.cpp}\n1502|    uint64_t\n1503|    @endcode\n1504|\n1505|    #### Default behavior\n1506|\n1507|    - The restrictions about leading zeros is not enforced in C++. Instead,\n1508|      leading zeros in integer literals lead to an interpretation as octal\n1509|      number. Internally, the value will be stored as decimal number. For\n1510|      instance, the C++ integer literal `010` will be serialized to `8`.\n1511|      During deserialization, leading zeros yield an error.\n1512|    - Not-a-number (NaN) values will be serialized to `null`.\n1513|\n1514|    #### Limits\n1515|\n1516|    [RFC 7159](http://rfc7159.net/rfc7159) specifies:\n1517|    > An implementation may set limits on the range and precision of numbers.\n1518|\n1519|    When the default type is used, the maximal integer number that can be\n1520|    stored is `18446744073709551615` (UINT64_MAX) and the minimal integer\n1521|    number that can be stored is `0`. Integer numbers that are out of range\n1522|    will yield over/underflow when used in a constructor. During\n1523|    deserialization, too large or small integer numbers will be automatically\n1524|    be stored as @ref number_integer_t or @ref number_float_t.\n1525|\n1526|    [RFC 7159](http://rfc7159.net/rfc7159) further states:\n1527|    > Note that when such software is used, numbers that are integers and are\n1528|    > in the range \\f$[-2^{53}+1, 2^{53}-1]\\f$ are interoperable in the sense\n1529|    > that implementations will agree exactly on their numeric values.\n1530|\n1531|    As this range is a subrange (when considered in conjunction with the\n1532|    number_integer_t type) of the exactly supported range [0, UINT64_MAX],\n1533|    this class's integer type is interoperable.\n1534|\n1535|    #### Storage\n1536|\n1537|    Integer number values are stored directly inside a @ref basic_json type.\n1538|\n1539|    @sa @ref number_float_t -- type for number values (floating-point)\n1540|    @sa @ref number_integer_t -- type for number values (integer)\n1541|\n1542|    @since version 2.0.0\n1543|    */\n1544|    using number_unsigned_t = NumberUnsignedType;\n1545|\n1546|    /*!\n1547|    @brief a type for a number (floating-point)\n1548|\n1549|    [RFC 7159](http://rfc7159.net/rfc7159) describes numbers as follows:\n1550|    > The representation of numbers is similar to that used in most\n1551|    > programming languages. A number is represented in base 10 using decimal\n1552|    > digits. It contains an integer component that may be prefixed with an\n1553|    > optional minus sign, which may be followed by a fraction part and/or an\n1554|    > exponent part. Leading zeros are not allowed. (...) Numeric values that\n1555|    > cannot be represented in the grammar below (such as Infinity and NaN)\n1556|    > are not permitted.\n1557|\n1558|    This description includes both integer and floating-point numbers.\n1559|    However, C++ allows more precise storage if it is known whether the number\n1560|    is a signed integer, an unsigned integer or a floating-point number.\n1561|    Therefore, three different types, @ref number_integer_t, @ref\n1562|    number_unsigned_t and @ref number_float_t are used.\n1563|\n1564|    To store floating-point numbers in C++, a type is defined by the template\n1565|    parameter @a NumberFloatType which chooses the type to use.\n1566|\n1567|    #### Default type\n1568|\n1569|    With the default values for @a NumberFloatType (`double`), the default\n1570|    value for @a number_float_t is:\n1571|\n1572|    @code {.cpp}\n1573|    double\n1574|    @endcode\n1575|\n1576|    #### Default behavior\n1577|\n1578|    - The restrictions about leading zeros is not enforced in C++. Instead,\n1579|      leading zeros in floating-point literals will be ignored. Internally,\n1580|      the value will be stored as decimal number. For instance, the C++\n1581|      floating-point literal `01.2` will be serialized to `1.2`. During\n1582|      deserialization, leading zeros yield an error.\n1583|    - Not-a-number (NaN) values will be serialized to `null`.\n1584|\n1585|    #### Limits\n1586|\n1587|    [RFC 7159](http://rfc7159.net/rfc7159) states:\n1588|    > This specification allows implementations to set limits on the range and\n1589|    > precision of numbers accepted. Since software that implements IEEE\n1590|    > 754-2008 binary64 (double precision) numbers is generally available and\n1591|    > widely used, good interoperability can be achieved by implementations\n1592|    > that expect no more precision or range than these provide, in the sense\n1593|    > that implementations will approximate JSON numbers within the expected\n1594|    > precision.\n1595|\n1596|    This implementation does exactly follow this approach, as it uses double\n1597|    precision floating-point numbers. Note values smaller than\n1598|    `-1.79769313486232e+308` and values greater than `1.79769313486232e+308`\n1599|    will be stored as NaN internally and be serialized to `null`.\n1600|\n1601|    #### Storage\n1602|\n1603|    Floating-point number values are stored directly inside a @ref basic_json\n1604|    type.\n1605|\n1606|    @sa @ref number_integer_t -- type for number values (integer)\n1607|\n1608|    @sa @ref number_unsigned_t -- type for number values (unsigned integer)\n1609|\n1610|    @since version 1.0.0\n1611|    */\n1612|    using number_float_t = NumberFloatType;\n1613|\n1614|    /// @}\n1615|\n1616|  private:\n1617|\n1618|    /// helper for exception-safe object creation\n1619|    template<typename T, typename... Args>\n1620|    static T* create(Args&& ... args)\n1621|    {\n1622|        AllocatorType<T> alloc;\n1623|        auto deleter = [&](T * object)\n1624|        {\n1625|            alloc.deallocate(object, 1);\n1626|        };\n1627|        std::unique_ptr<T, decltype(deleter)> object(alloc.allocate(1), deleter);\n1628|        alloc.construct(object.get(), std::forward<Args>(args)...);\n1629|        assert(object != nullptr);\n1630|        return object.release();\n1631|    }\n1632|\n1633|    ////////////////////////\n1634|    // JSON value storage //\n1635|    ////////////////////////\n1636|\n1637|    /*!\n1638|    @brief a JSON value\n1639|\n1640|    The actual storage for a JSON value of the @ref basic_json class. The size of the union should not exceed 64 bits if the default\n1657|    value types are used.\n1658|\n1659|    @since version 1.0.0\n1660|    */\n1661|    union json_value\n1662|    {\n1663| value_t::boolean:\n1712|                {\n1713|                    boolean = boolean_t(false);\n1714|                    break;\n1715|                }\n1716|\n1717|                case      case value_t::null:\n1736|                {\n1737|                    break;\n1738|                }\n1739|\n1740|                default:\n1741|                {\n1742|                    if (t create<array_t>(value);\n1767|        }\n1768|    };\n1769|\n1770|    /*!\n1771|    @brief checks the class invariants\n1772|\n1773|    This function asserts the class invariants. It needs to be called at the\n1774|    end of every constructor to make sure that created objects respect the\n1775|    invariant. Furthermore, it has to be called each time the type of a JSON\n1776|    value is changed, because the invariant expresses a relationship between\n1777|    @a m_type and @a m_value.\n1778|    */\n1779|    void assert_invariant() const\n1780|    {\n1781|        assert(m_type != value_t::object or m_value.object != nullptr);\n1782|        assert(m_type != value_t::array or m_value.array != nullptr);\n1783|        assert(m_type != value_t::string or m_value.string != nullptr);\n1784|    }\n1785|\n1786|  public:\n1787|    //////////////////////////\n1788|    // JSON parser callback //\n1789|    //////////////////////////\n1790|\n1791|    /*!\n1792|    @brief JSON callback events\n1793|\n1794|    This enumeration lists the parser events that can trigger calling a\n1795|    callback function of type @ref parser_callback_t during parsing.\n1796|\n1797|    @image html callback_events.png \"Example when certain parse events are triggered\"\n1798|\n1799|    @since version 1.0.0\n1800|    */\n1801|    enum class parse_event_t : uint8_t\n1802|    {\n1803|        /// the parser read `{` and started to process a JSON object\n1804|        object_start,\n1805|        /// the parser read `}` and finished processing a JSON object\n1806|        object_end,\n1807|        /// the parser read `[` and started to process a JSON array\n1808|        array_start,\n1809|        /// the parser read `]` and finished processing a JSON array\n1810|        array_end,\n1811|        /// the parser read a key of a value in an object\n1812|        key,\n1813|        /// the parser finished reading a JSON value\n1814|        value\n1815|    };\n1816|\n1817|    /*!\n1818|    @brief per-element parser callback type\n1819|\n1820|    With a parser callback function, the result of parsing a JSON text can be\n1821|    influenced. When passed to @ref parse(std::istream&, const\n1822|    parser_callback_t) or @ref parse(const CharT, const parser_callback_t),\n1823|    it is called on certain events (passed as @ref parse_event_t via parameter\n1824|    @a event) with a set recursion depth @a depth and context JSON value\n1825|    @a parsed. The return value of the callback function is a boolean\n1826|    indicating whether the element that emitted the callback shall be kept or\n1827|    not.\n1828|\n1829|    We distinguish six scenarios (determined by the event type) in which the\n1830|    callback function can be called. The following table describes the values\n1831|    of the parameters @a depth, @a event, and @a parsed.\n1832|\n1833|    parameter @a event | description | parameter @a depth | parameter @a parsed\n1834|    ------------------ | ----------- | ------------------ | -------------------\n1835|    parse_event_t::object_start | the parser read `{` and started to process a JSON object | depth of the parent of the JSON object | a JSON value with type discarded\n1836|    parse_event_t::key | the parser read a key of a value in an object | depth of the currently parsed JSON object | a JSON string containing the key\n1837|    parse_event_t::object_end | the parser read `}` and finished processing a JSON object | depth of the parent of the JSON object | the parsed JSON object\n1838|    parse_event_t::array_start | the parser read `[` and started to process a JSON array | depth of the parent of the JSON array | a JSON value with type discarded\n1839|    parse_event_t::array_end | the parser read `]` and finished processing a JSON array | depth of the parent of the JSON array | the parsed JSON array\n1840|    parse_event_t::value | the parser finished reading a JSON value | depth of the value | the parsed JSON value\n1841|\n1842|    @image html callback_events.png \"Example when certain parse events are triggered\"\n1843|\n1844|    Discarding a value (i.e., returning `false`) has different effects\n1845|    depending on the context in which function was called:\n1846|\n1847|    - Discarded values in structured types are skipped. That is, the parser\n1848|      will behave as if the discarded value was never read.\n1849|    - In case a value outside a structured type is skipped, it is replaced\n1850|      with `null`. This case happens if the top-level element is skipped.\n1851|\n1852|    @param[in] depth  the depth of the recursion during parsing\n1853|\n1854|    @param[in] event  an event of type parse_event_t indicating the context in\n1855|    the callback function has been called\n1856|\n1857|    @param[in,out] parsed  the current intermediate parse result; note that\n1858|    writing to this value has no effect for parse_event_t::key events\n1859|\n1860|    @return Whether the JSON value which called the function during parsing\n1861|    should be kept (`true`) or not (`false`). In the latter case, it is either\n1862|    skipped completely or replaced by an empty discarded object.\n1863|\n1864|    @sa @ref parse(std::istream&, parser_callback_t) or\n1865|    @ref parse(const CharT, const parser_callback_t) for examples\n1866|\n1867|    @since version 1.0.0\n1868|    */\n1869|    using parser_callback_t = std::function<bool(int depth,\n1870|                              parse_event_t event,\n1871|                              basic_json& parsed)>;\n1872|\n1873|\n1874|    //////////////////\n1875|    // constructors //\n1876|    //////////////////\n1877|\n1878|    /// @name constructors and destructors\n1879|    /// Constructors of class @ref basic_json, copy/move constructor, copy\n1880|    /// assignment, static functions creating objects, and the destructor.\n1881|    /// @{\n1882|\n1883|    /*!\n1884|    @brief create an empty value with a given type\n1885|\n1886|    Create an empty JSON value with a given type. The constructor forwards the\n1945|    parameter @a val to that method (to `json_serializer<U>::to_json` method\n1946|    with `U = uncvref_t<CompatibleType>`, to be exact).\n1947|\n1948|    Template type @a CompatibleType includes, but is not limited to, the\n1949|    following types:\n1950|    - **arrays**: @ref array_t and all kinds of compatible containers such as\n1951|      `std::vector`, `std::deque`, `std::list`, `std::forward_list`,\n1952|      `std::array`, `std::set`, `std::unordered_set`, `std::multiset`, and\n1953|      `unordered_multiset` with a `value_type` from which a @ref basic_json\n1954|      value can be constructed.\n1955|    - **objects**: @ref object_t and all kinds of compatible associative\n1956|      containers such as `std::map`, `std::unordered_map`, `std::multimap`,\n1957|      and `std::unordered_multimap` with a `key_type` compatible to\n1958|      @ref string_t and a `value_type` from which a @ref basic_json value can\n1959|      be constructed.\n1960|    - **strings**: @ref string_t, string literals, and all compatible string\n1961|      containers can be used.\n1962|    - **numbers**: @ref number_integer_t, @ref number_unsigned_t,\n1963|      @ref number_float_t, and all convertible number types such as `int`,\n1964|      `size_t`, `int64_t`, `float` or `double` can be used.\n1965|    - **boolean**: @ref boolean_t / `bool` can be used.\n1966|\n1967|    See the examples below.\n1968|\n1969|    @tparam CompatibleType a type such that:\n1970|    - @a CompatibleType is not derived from `std::istream`,\n1971|    - @a CompatibleType is not @ref basic_json (to avoid hijacking copy/move\n1972|         constructors),\n1973|    - @a CompatibleType is not a @ref basic_json nested type (e.g.,\n1974|         @ref json_pointer, @ref iterator, etc ...)\n1975|    - In case @a type_deduction is `true` (default), the type of\n2012|    the JSON value to be created is deducted from the initializer list @a init\n2013|    according to the following rules:\n2014|\n2015|    1. If the list is empty, an empty JSON object value `{}` is created.\n2016|    2. If the list consists of pairs whose first element is a string, a JSON\n2017|       object value is created where the first elements of the pairs are\n2018|       treated as keys and the second elements are as values.\n2019|    3. In all other cases, an array is created.\n2020|\n2021|    The rules aim to create the best fit between a C++ initializer list and\n2022|    JSON values. The rationale is as follows:\n2023|\n2024|    1. The empty initializer list is written as `{}` which is exactly an empty\n2025|       JSON object.\n2026|    2. C++ has now way of describing mapped types other than to list a list of\n2027|       pairs. As JSON requires that keys must be of type string, rule 2 is the\n2028|       weakest constraint one can pose on initializer lists to interpret them\n2029|       as an object.\n2030|    3. In all other cases, the initializer list could not be interpreted as\n2031|       JSON object type, so interpreting it as JSON array type is safe.\n2032|\n2033|    With the rules described above, the following JSON values cannot be\n2034|    expressed by an initializer list:\n2035|\n2036|    - the empty array (`[]`): use @ref array(std::initializer_list<basic_json>)\n2037|      with an empty initializer list in this case\n2038|    - arrays whose elements satisfy rule 2: use @ref\n2039|      array(std::initializer_list<basic_json>) with the same initializer list\n2040|      in this case\n2041|\n2042|    @note When used without parentheses around an empty initializer list, @ref\n2043|    basic_json() is called instead of this function, yielding the JSON null\n2044|    value.\n2045|\n2046|    @param[in] init  initializer list with JSON values\n2047|\n2048|    @param[in] type_deduction internal parameter; when set to `true`, the type\n2049|    of the JSON value is deducted from the initializer list @a init; when set\n2050|    to `false`, the type provided via @a manual_type is forced. This mode is\n2051|    used by the functions @ref array(std::initializer_list<basic_json>) and\n2052|    @ref object(std::initializer_list<basic_json>).\n2053|\n2054|    @param[in] manual_type internal parameter; when @a type_deduction is set\n2055|    to `false`, the created JSON value will use the provided type (only @ref\n2056|    value_t::array and @ref value_t::object are valid); when @a type_deduction\n2057|    is set to `true`, this parameter has no effect\n2058|\n2059|    @throw std::domain_error if @a type_deduction is `false`, @a manual_type\n2060|    is `value_t::object`, but @a init contains an element which is not a pair\n2061|    whose first element is a string; example: `\"cannot create object from\n2062|    initializer list\"`\n2063|\n2064|    @complexity Linear in the size of the initializer list @a init.\n2065|\n2066|    @liveexample{The example below shows how JSON values are created from\n2067|    initializer lists.,basic_json__list_init_t}\n2068|\n2069|    @sa @ref array(std::initializer_list<basic_json>) -- create a JSON array\n2070|    value from an initializer list\n2071|    @sa @ref object(std::initializer_list<basic_json>) -- create a JSON object\n2072|    value from an initializer list\n2073|\n2074|    @since version 1.0.0\n2075|    */\n2076|    basic_json(std::initializer_list<basic_json> init,\n2077|               bool type_deduction = true,\n2078|               value_t manual_type = value_t::array)\n2079|    {\n2080|        // check if each element is an array with two elements whose first\n2081|        // element is a string\n2082|        bool is_an_object = std::all_of(init.begin(), init.end(),\n2083|                                        [](const basic_json & element)\n2084|        {\n2085|            return element.is_array() and element.size() == 2 and element[0].is_string();\n2086|        });\n2087|\n2088|        // adjust type if type deduction is not wanted\n2089|        if (not type_deduction)\n2090|        {\n2091|            // if array is wanted, do not create an object though possible\n2092|            if (manual_type == value_t::array)\n2093|            {\n2094|                is_an_object = false;\n2095|            }\n2096|\n2097|            // if object is wanted but impossible, throw an exception\n2098|            if (manual_type == value_t::object and not is_an_object)\n2099|            {\n2100|                JSON_THROW(std::domain_error(\"cannot create object from initializer list\"));\n2101|            }\n2102|        }\n2103|\n2104|        if (is_an_object)\n2105|        {\n2106|            // the initializer list is a list of pairs -> create object\n2107|            m_type = value_t::object;\n2108|            m_value = value_t::object;\n2109|\n2110|            std::for_each(init.begin(), init.end(), [this](const basic_json & element)\n2111|            {\n2112|                m_value.object->emplace(*(element[0].m_value.string), element[1]);\n2113|            });\n2114|        }\n2115|        else\n2116|        {\n2117|            // the initializer list describes an array -> create array\n2118|            m_type = value_t::array;\n2119|            m_value.array = create<array_t>(init);\n2120|        }\n2121|\n2122|        assert_invariant();\n2123|    }\n2124|\n2125|    /*!\n2126|    @brief explicitly create an array from an initializer list\n2127|\n2128|    Creates a JSON array value from a given initializer list. That is, given a\n2129|    list of values `a, b, c`, creates the JSON value `[a, b, c]`. If the\n2130|    initializer list is empty, the empty array `[]` is created.\n2131|\n2132|    @note This function is only needed to express two edge cases that cannot\n2133|    be realized with the initializer list constructor (@ref\n2134|    basic_json(std::initializer_list<basic_json>, bool, value_t)). These cases\n2135|    are:\n2136|    1. creating an array whose elements are all pairs whose first element is a\n2137|    string -- in this case, the initializer list constructor would create an\n2138|    object, taking the first elements as keys\n2139|    2. creating an empty array -- passing the empty initializer list to the\n2140|    initializer list constructor yields an empty object\n2141|\n2142|    @param[in] init  initializer list with JSON values to create an array from\n2143|    (optional)\n2144|\n2145|    @return JSON array value\n2146|\n2147|    @complexity Linear in the size of @a init.\n2148|\n2149|    @liveexample{The following code shows an example for the `array`\n2150|    function.,array}\n2151|\n2152|    @sa @ref basic_json(std::initializer_list<basic_json>, bool, value_t) --\n2153|    create a JSON value from an initializer list\n2154|    @sa @ref object(std::initializer_list<basic_json>) -- create a JSON object\n2155|    value from an initializer list\n2156|\n2157|    @since version 1.0.0\n2158|    */\n2159|    static basic_json array(std::initializer_list<basic_json> init =\n2160|                                std::initializer_list<basic_json>())\n2161|    {\n2162|        return basic_json(init, false, value_t::array);\n2163|    }\n2164|\n2165|    /*!\n2166|    @brief explicitly create an object from an initializer list\n2167|\n2168|    Creates a JSON object value from a given initializer list. The initializer\n2169|    lists elements must be pairs, and their first elements must be strings. If\n2170|    the initializer list is empty, the empty object `{}` is created.\n2171|\n2172|    @note This function is only added for symmetry reasons. In contrast to the\n2173|    related function @ref array(std::initializer_list<basic_json>), there are\n2174|    no cases which can only be expressed by this function. That is, any\n2175|    initializer list @a init can also be passed to the initializer list\n2176|    constructor @ref basic_json(std::initializer_list<basic_json>, bool,\n2177|    value_t).\n2178|\n2179|    @param[in] init  initializer list to create an object from (optional)\n2180|\n2181|    @return JSON object value\n2182|\n2183|    @throw std::domain_error if @a init is not a pair whose first elements are\n2184|    strings; thrown by\n2185|    @ref basic_json(std::initializer_list<basic_json>, bool, value_t)\n2186|\n2187|    @complexity Linear in the size of @a init.\n2188|\n2189|    @liveexample{The following code shows an example for the `object`\n2190|    function.,object}\n2191|\n2192|    @sa @ref basic_json(std::initializer_list<basic_json>, bool, value_t) --\n2193|    create a JSON value from an initializer list\n2194|    @sa @ref array(std::initializer_list<basic_json>) -- create a JSON array\n2195|    value from an initializer list\n2196|\n2197|    @since version 1.0.0\n2198|    */\n2199|    static basic_json object(std::initializer_list<basic_json> init =\n2200|                                 std::initializer_list<basic_json>())\n2201|    {\n2202|        return basic_json(init, false, value_t::object);\n2203|    }\n2204|\n2205|    /*!\n2206|    @brief construct an array with count copies of given value\n2207|\n2208|    Constructs a JSON array value by creating @a cnt copies of a passed value.\n2209|    In case @a cnt is `0`, an empty array is created. In this case, the value is\n2237|      copied. Otherwise, std::out_of_range is thrown.\n2238|    - In case of structured types (array, object), the constructor behaves as\n2239|      similar versions for `std::vector`.\n2240|    - In case of a      }\n2280|\n2281|        // copy type from first iterator\n2282|        m_type = first.m_object->m_type;\n2283|\n2284|        // check if iterator range is complete for primitive values\n2285| {\n5854|                    return *lhs.m_value.array == *rhs.m_value.array;\n5855|                }\n5856|                case value_t::object:\n5857|                {\n5858|                    return *lhs.m_value.object == *rhs.m_value.object;\n5859|                }\n5860|                case value_t::null:\n5861|                {\n5862|                    return true;\n5863|                }\n5864|                case value_t::string:\n5865|                {\n5866|                    return *lhs.m_value.string == *rhs.m_value.string;\n5867|                }\n5868|                case value_t::boolean:\n5869|                {\n5870|                    return lhs.m_value.boolean == rhs.m_value.boolean;\n5871|                }\n5872|                case value_t::number_integer:\n5873|                {\n5874|                    return lhs.m_value.number_integer == rhs.m_value.number_integer;\n5875|                }\n5876|                case value_t::number_unsigned:\n5877|                {\n5878|                    return lhs.m_value.number_unsigned == rhs.m_value.number_unsigned;\n5879|                }\n5880|                case value_t::number_float:\n5881|                {\n5882|                    return lhs.m_value.number_float == rhs.m_value.number_float;\n5883|                }\n5884|                default:\n5885| characters by a sequence of \"\\u\" followed by a four-digit hex\n6460|        representation. The escaped string is written to output stream @a o.\n6461|\n6462|        @param[in] s  the string to escape\n6463|\n6464|        @complexity Linear in the length of string @a s.\n6465| ++pos;\n6584|                        break;\n6585|                    }\n6586|\n6587|                    default:\n6588|                    {\n6589|                        // all other characters are assert(pos == s.size() + space);\n6597|            o.write(result.c_str(), static_cast<std::streamsize>(result.size()));\n6598|        }\n6599|\n6600|        /*!\n6601|        @brief dump an integer\n6602|\n6603|        Dump a given integer to output stream @a o. Works internally with\n6604|        @a number_buffer.\n6605|\n6606|        @param[in] x  integer number (signed or unsigned) to dump\n6607|        @tparam NumberType either @a number_integer_t or @a number_unsigned_t\n6608|        */\n6609|        template<typename NumberType, detail::enable_if_t <\n6610|                     std::is_same<NumberType, number_unsigned_t>::value or\n6611|                     std::is_same<NumberType, number_integer_t>::value, int> = 0>\n6612|        void dump_integer(NumberType x)\n6613|        {\n6614|            // special case for \"0\"\n6615|            if (x == 0)\n6616|            {\n6617|                o.put('0');\n6618|                return;\n6619|            }\n6620|\n6621|            const bool is_negative = x < 0;\n6622|            size_t i = 0;\n6623|\n6624|            // spare 1 byte for '\\0'\n6625|            while (x != 0 and i < number_buffer.size() - 1)\n6626|            {\n6627|                const auto digit = std::labs(static_cast<long>(x % 10));\n6628|                number_buffer[i++] = static_cast<char>('0' + digit);\n6629|                x /= 10;\n6630|            }\n6631|\n6632|            // make sure the number has been processed completely\n6633|            assert(x == 0);\n6634|\n6635|            if (is_negative)\n6636|            {\n6637|                // make sure there is capacity for the '-'\n6638|                assert(i < number_buffer.size() - 2);\n6639|                number_buffer[i++] = '-';\n6640|            }\n6641|\n6642|            std::reverse(number_buffer.begin(), number_buffer.begin() + i);\n6643|            o.write(number_buffer.data(), static_cast<std::streamsize>(i));\n6644|        }\n6645|\n6646|        /*!\n6647|        @brief dump a floating-point number\n6648|\n6649|        Dump a given floating-point number to output stream @a o. add_to_vector(std::vector<uint8_t>& vec, size_t bytes, const T number)\n7088|    {\n7089|        assert(bytes == 1 or bytes == 2 or bytes == 4 or bytes == 8);\n7090|\n7091|        switch            vec.push_back(static_cast<uint8_t>((static_cast<uint64_t>(number) >> 040) & 0xff));\n7099|                vec.push_back(static_cast<uint8_t>((number >> 030) & 0xff));\n7100| vec.push_back(static_cast<uint8_t>(number & 0xff));\n7103|                break;\n7104|            }\n7105|\n7106|            case 4:\n7107|            {\n7108| vec.push_back(static_cast<uint8_t>((number >> 010) & 0xff));\n7111|                vec.push_back(static_cast<uint8_t>(number & 0xff));\n7112|                break;\n7113|            }\n7114|\n7115| break;\n7126|            }\n7127|        }\n7128|    }\n7129|\n7130|    /*!\n7131|    @brief take sufficient bytes from a vector to fill an integer variable\n7132|\n7133|    In the context of binary serialization formats, we need to read several\n7134|    bytes from a byte vector and combine them to multi-byte integral data\n7135|    types.\n7136|\n7137|    @param[in] vec  byte vector to read from\n7138|    @param[in] current_index  the position in the vector after which to read\n7139|\n7140|    @return the next sizeof(T) bytes from @a vec, in reverse order as T\n7141|\n7142|    @tparam T the integral return type\n7143|\n7144|    @throw std::out_of_range if there are less than sizeof(T)+1 bytes in the\n7145|           vector @a vec to read\n7146|\n7147|    In the for loop, the bytes from the vector are copied in reverse order into\n7148|    the return value. In the figures below, let sizeof(T)=4 and `i` be the loop\n7149|    variable.\n7150|\n7151|    Precondition:\n7152|\n7153|    vec:   |   |   | a | b | c | d |      T: |   |   |   |   |\n7154|                 ^               ^             ^                ^\n7155|           current_index         i            ptr        sizeof(T)\n7156|\n7157|    Postcondition:\n7158|\n7159|    vec:   |   |   | a | b | c | d |      T: | d | c | b | a |\n7160|                 ^   ^                                     ^\n7161|                 |   i                                    ptr\n7162|           current_index\n7163|\n7164|    @sa Code adapted from <http://stackoverflow.com/a/41031865/266378>.\n7165|    */\n7166|    template<typename T>\n7167|    static T get_from_vector(const std::vector<uint8_t>& vec, const size_t current_index)\n7168|    {\n7169|        if (current_index + sizeof(T) + 1 > vec.size())\n7170|        {\n7171|            JSON_THROW(std::out_of_range(\"cannot read \" + std::to_string(sizeof(T)) + \" bytes from vector\"));\n7172|        }\n7173|\n7174|        T result;\n7175|        auto* ptr = reinterpret_cast<uint8_t*>(&result);\n7176|        for (size_t i = 0; i < sizeof(T); ++i)\n7177|        {\n7178|            *ptr++ = vec[current_index + sizeof(T) - i];\n7179|        }\n7180|        return result;\n7181|    }\n7182|\n7183|    /*!\n7184|    @brief create a MessagePack serialization of a given JSON value\n7185|\n7186|    This is a straightforward implementation of the MessagePack specification.\n7187|\n7188|    @param[in] j  JSON value to serialize\n7189|    @param[in,out] v  byte vector to write the serialization to\n7190|\n7191|    @sa https://github.com/msgpack/msgpack/blob/master/spec.md\n7192|    */\n7193|    static void to_msgpack_internal(const basic_json& j, std::vector<uint8_t>& v)\n7194|    {\n7195|        switch (j.type())\n7196|        {\n7197|            case value_t::null:\n7198|            {\n7199|                // nil\n7200|                v.push_back(0xc0);\n7201|                break;\n7202|            }\n7203|\n7204|            case value_t::boolean:\n7205|            {\n7206|                // true and false\n7207|                v.push_back(j.m_value.boolean ? 0xc3 : 0xc2);\n7208|                break;\n7209|            }\n7210|\n7211|            case value_t::number_integer:\n7212|            {\n7213|                if (j.m_value.number_integer >= 0)\n7214|                {\n7215|                    // MessagePack does not differentiate between positive\n7216|                    // signed integers and unsigned integers.                    {\n7221|                        // positive fixnum\n7222|                        add_to_vector(v, 1, j.m_value.number_unsigned);\n7223|                    }\n7224|                       add_to_vector(v, 1, j.m_value.number_unsigned);\n7229|                    }\n7230|                    else if (j.m_value.number_unsigned <= a given vector\n7696|\n7697|    To secure the access to the byte vector during CBOR/MessagePack\n7698|    deserialization, bytes are copied from the vector into buffers. This\n7699|    function checks if the number of bytes to copy (@a len) does not exceed\n7700|    the size @s size of the vector. Additionally, an @a offset is given from\n7701|    where to start reading the bytes.\n7702|\n7703|    This function checks whether reading the bytes is safe; that is, offset is\n7704|    a valid index in the vector, offset+len\n7705|\n7706|    @param[in] size    size of the byte vector\n7707|    @param[in] len     number of bytes to read\n7708|    @param[in] offset  offset where to start reading\n7709|\n7710|    vec:  x x x x x X X X X X\n7711|          ^         ^         ^\n7712|          0         offset    len\n7713|\n7714|    @throws out_of_range if `len > v.size()`\n7715|    */\n7716|    static void check_length(const size_t size, const size_t len, const size_t offset)\n7717|    {\n7718|        // simple case: requested length is greater than the vector's length\n7719|        if (len > size or offset > size)\n7720|        {\n7721|            JSON_THROW(std::out_of_range(\"len out of range\"));\n7722|        }\n7723|\n7724|        // second case: adding offset would result in overflow\n7725|        if ((size > (std::numeric_limits<size_t>::max() - offset)))\n7726|        {\n7727|            JSON_THROW(std::out_of_range(\"len+offset out of range\"));\n7728|        }\n7729|\n7730|        // last case: reading past the end of the vector\n7731|        if (len + offset > size)\n7732|        {\n7733|            JSON_THROW(std::out_of_range(\"len+offset out of range\"));\n7734|        }\n7735|    }\n7736|\n7737|    /*!\n7738|    @brief check if the next byte belongs to a string\n7739|\n7740|    While parsing a map, the keys must be strings. This function checks if the\n7741|    current byte is one of the start bytes for a string in MessagePack:\n7742|\n7743|    - 0xa0 - 0xbf: fixstr\n7744|    - 0xd9: str 8\n7745|    - 0xda: str 16\n7746|    - 0xdb: str 32\n7747|\n7748|    @param[in] v  MessagePack serialization\n7749|    @param[in] idx  byte index in @a v to check for a string\n7750|\n7751|    @throw std::invalid_argument if `v[idx]` basic_json result = value_t::object;\n8364|                const auto len = static_cast<size_t>(get_from_vector<uint16_t>(v, current_idx));\n8365|                idx += 2; // skip 2 size bytes\n8366|                for (size_t i = 0; i < len; ++i)\n8367|                {\n8368|                    std::string key = from_cbor_internal(v, idx);\n8369|                    result[key] = from_cbor_internal(v, idx);\n8370|                }\n8371|                return result;\n8372|            }\n8373|\n8374|            case 0xba: // map (four-byte uint32_t for n follow)\n8375|            {\n8376|                basic_json result = value_t::object;\n8377|                const auto len = static_cast<size_t>(get_from_vector<uint32_t>(v, current_idx));\n8378|                idx += 4; // skip 4 size bytes\n8379|                for (size_t i = 0; i < len; ++i)\n8380|                {\n8381|                    std::string key = from_cbor_internal(v, idx);\n8382|                    result[key] = from_cbor_internal(v, idx);\n8383|                }\n8384|                return result;\n8385|            }\n8386|\n8387|            case 0xbb: // map (eight-byte uint64_t for n follow)\n8388|            {\n8389|                basic_json result = value_t::object;\n8390|                const auto len = static_cast<size_t>(get_from_vector<uint64_t>(v, current_idx));\n8391|                idx += 8; // skip 8 size bytes\n8392|                for (size_t i = 0; i < len; ++i)\n8393|                {\n8394|                    std::string key = from_cbor_internal(v, idx);\n8395|                    result[key] = from_cbor_internal(v, idx);\n8396|                }\n8397|                return result;\n8398|            }\n8399|\n8400|            case 0xbf: // map (indefinite length)\n8401|            {\n8402|                basic_json result = value_t::object;\n8403|                while (v.at(idx) != 0xff)\n8404|                {\n8405|                    std::string key = from_cbor_internal(v, idx);\n8406|                    result[key] = from_cbor_internal(v, idx);\n8407|                }\n8408|                // skip break byte (0xFF)\n8409|                idx += 1;\n8410|                return result;\n8411|            }\n8412|\n8413|            case 0xf4: // false\n8414|            {\n8415|                return false;\n8416|            }\n8417|\n8418|            case 0xf5: // true\n8419|            {\n8420|                return true;\n8421|            }\n8422|\n8423|            case 0xf6: // null\n8424|            {\n8425|                return value_t::null;\n8426|            }\n8427|\n8428|            case 0xf9: // Half-Precision Float (two-byte IEEE 754)\n8429|            {\n8430|                idx += 2; // skip two content bytes\n8431|\n8432|                // code from RFC 7049, Appendix D, Figure 3:\n8433|                // As half-precision floating-point numbers were only added to\n8434|                // IEEE 754 in 2008, today's programming platforms often still\n8435|                // only have limited support for them. It is very easy to\n8436|                // include at least decoding support for them even without such\n8437|                // support. An example of a small decoder for half-precision\n8438|                // floating-point numbers in the C language is shown in Fig. 3.\n8439|                const int half = (v.at(current_idx + 1) << 8) + v.at(current_idx + 2);\n8440|                const int exp = (half >> 10) & 0x1f;\n8441|                const int mant = half & 0x3ff;\n8442|                double val;\n8443|                if (exp == 0)\n8444|                {\n8445|                    val = std::ldexp(mant, -24);\n8446|                }\n8447|                else if (exp != 31)\n8448|                {\n8449|                    val = std::ldexp(mant + 1024, exp - 25);\n8450|                }\n8451|                else\n8452|                {\n8453|                    val = mant == 0\n8454|                          ? std::numeric_limits<double>::infinity()\n8455|                          : std::numeric_limits<double>::quiet_NaN();\n8456|                }\n8457|                return (half & 0x8000) != 0 ? -val : val;\n8458|            }\n8459|\n8460|            case 0xfa: // Single-Precision Float (four-byte IEEE 754)\n8461|            {\n8462|                // copy bytes in reverse order into the float variable\n8463|                float res;\n8464|                for (size_t byte = 0; byte < sizeof(float); ++byte)\n8465|                {\n8466|                    reinterpret_cast<uint8_t*>(&res)[sizeof(float) - byte - 1] = v.at(current_idx + 1 + byte);\n8467|                }\n8468|                idx += sizeof(float); // skip content bytes\n8469|                return res;\n8470|            }\n8471|\n8472|            case 0xfb: // Double-Precision Float (eight-byte IEEE 754)\n8473|            {\n8474|                // copy bytes in reverse order into the double variable\n8475|                double res;\n8476|                for (size_t byte = 0; byte < sizeof(double); ++byte)\n8477|                {\n8478|                    reinterpret_cast<uint8_t*>(&res)[sizeof(double) - byte - 1] = v.at(current_idx + 1 + byte);\n8479|                }\n8480|                idx += sizeof(double); // skip content bytes\n8481|                return res;\n8482|            }\n8483|\n8484|            default: // anything else (0xFF is handled inside the other types)\n8485|            {\n8486|                JSON_THROW(std::invalid_argument(\"error parsing a CBOR @ \" + std::to_string(current_idx) + \": \" + std::to_string(static_cast<int>(v[current_idx]))));\n8487|            }\n8488|        }\n8489|    }\n8490|\n8491|  public:\n8492|    /*!\n8493|    @brief create a MessagePack serialization of a given JSON value\n8494|\n8495|    Serializes a given JSON value @a j to a byte vector using the MessagePack\n8496|    serialization format. /*!\n8680|    @brief an iterator for primitive JSON types\n8681|\n8682|    This class models an iterator for primitive JSON types (boolean, number,\n8683|    string). {\n8727|            return !(lhs == rhs);\n8728|        }\n8729|\n8730|        friend constexpr bool operator<(primitive_iterator_t lhs, primitive_iterator_t rhs) noexcept\n8731|        {\n8732|            return lhs.m_it < rhs.m_it;\n8733|        }\n8734|\n8735|        friend constexpr bool operator<=(primitive_iterator_t lhs, primitive_iterator_t rhs) noexcept\n8736|        {\n8737| /*!\n8814|    @brief an iterator value\n8815|\n8816|    @note This structure could easily be a union, but MSVC currently does not\n8817|    allow unions members with complex constructors, see\n8818| @brief a template for a random access iterator for the @ref basic_json class\n8934|\n8935|    This class implements a both iterators (iterator and const_iterator) for the\n8936|    @ref basic_json class.\n8937|\n8938|    @note An iterator is called *initialized* when a pointer to a JSON value\n8939|          has been set (e.g., by a constructor or a copy assignment). If the\n8940|          iterator is default-constructed, it is *uninitialized* and most\n8941|          methods are undefined. **The library uses assertions to detect calls\n8942|          on uninitialized iterators.**\n8943|\n8944|    @requirement The class satisfies the following concept requirements:\n8945|    - [RandomAccessIterator](http://en.cppreference.com/w/cpp/concept/RandomAccessIterator):\n8946|      The iterator that can be moved to point (forward and backward) to any\n8947|      element in constant time.\n8948|\n8949|    @since version 1.0.0, simplified in version 2.0.9\n8950|    */\n8951|    template<typename U>\n8952|    class iter_impl : public std::iterator<std::random_access_iterator_tag, U>\n8953|    {\n8954|        /// allow basic_json to access private members\n8955|        friend class basic_json;\n8956|\n8957|        // make sure U is basic_json or const basic_json\n8958|        static_assert(std::is_same<U, basic_json>::value\n8959|                      or std::is_same<U, const basic_json>::value,\n8960|                      \"iter_impl only accepts (const) basic_json\");\n8961|\n8962|      public:\n8963|        /// the type of the values when the iterator is dereferenced\n8964|        using value_type = typename basic_json::value_type;\n8965|        /// (value_type)\n8968|        using pointer = typename std::conditional<std::is_const<U>::value,\n8969|              typename basic_json::const_pointer,\n8970|              typename basic_json::pointer>::type;\n8971|        /// defines a reference to the type iterated over (value_type)\n8972|        using reference = typename std::conditional<std::is_const<U>::value,\n8973|              typename basic_json::const_reference,\n8974|              typename basic_json::reference>::type;\n8975|        /// the category of the iterator\n8976|        using iterator_category = instance\n8983|        @param[in] object  pointer to a JSON object for this iterator\n8984|        @pre object != nullptr\n8985|        @post The iterator is initialized; i.e. `m_object != nullptr`.\n8986|        */\n8987|        explicit iter_impl(pointer object) noexcept\n8988|            : m_object(object)\n8989|        {\n8990|            assert(m_object != nullptr);\n8991|\n8992|            switch (m_object->m_type)\n8993|            {\n8994|                case basic_json::value_t::object:\n8995|                {\n8996|                    m_it.object_iterator = typename m_it.array_iterator = typename array_t::iterator();\n9003|                    break;\n9004|                }\n9005|\n9006|                default:\n9007|                {\n9008|                    m_it.primitive_iterator = primitive_iterator_t();\n9009|                    break;\n9010|                }\n9011|            }\n9012|        }\n9013|\n9014|        /*\n9015|        Use operator two cases: (1) @a codepoint1 is in the Basic Multilingual\n9741|        Plane (U+0000 through U+FFFF) and @a codepoint2 is 0, or (2)\n9742|        @a codepoint1 and @a codepoint2 are a UTF-16 point (can be low surrogate or 0)\n9747|\n9748|        @return string representation of the code point; the length of the\n9749|        result string is between 1 and 4 characters.\n9750|\n9751|        @throw std::out_of_range if code point is > 0x10ffff; example: `\"code\n9752|        points above 0x10FFFF are invalid\"`\n9753|        @throw std::invalid_argument if the low surrogate is invalid; example:\n9754|        `\"\"missing or wrong low surrogate\"\"`\n9755|\n9756|        @complexity Constant.\n9757|\n9758|        @see <http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UTF-8#Sample_code>\n9759|        */\n9760|        static string_t to_unicode(const std::size_t codepoint1,\n9761|                                   const std::size_t codepoint2 = 0)\n9762|        {\n9763|            // calculate the code point from the given code points\n9764|            std::size_t codepoint = codepoint1;\n9765|\n9766|            // check if codepoint1 is a high surrogate\n9767|            if (codepoint1 >= 0xD800 and codepoint1 <= 0xDBFF)\n9768|            {\n9769|                // check if codepoint2 is a low surrogate\n9770|                if (codepoint2 >= 0xDC00 and codepoint2 <= 0xDFFF)\n9771|                {\n9772|                    codepoint =\n9773|                        // high surrogate occupies the most significant 22 bits\n9774|                        (codepoint1 << 10)\n9775|                        // low surrogate occupies the least significant 15 bits\n9776|                        + codepoint2\n9777|                        // there is still the 0xD800, 0xDC00 and 0x10000 noise\n9778|                        // in the result so we have to subtract with:\n9779|                        // (0xD800 << 10) + DC00 - 0x10000 = 0x35FDC00\n9780|                        - 0x35FDC00;\n9781|                }\n9782|                else\n9783|                {\n9784|                    JSON_THROW(std::invalid_argument(\"missing or wrong low surrogate\"));\n9785|                }\n9786|            }\n9787|\n9788|            string_t result;\n9789|\n9790|            if (codepoint < 0x80)\n9791|            {\n9792|                // 1-byte characters: 0xxxxxxx (ASCII)\n9793|                result.append(1, static_cast<typename string_t::value_type>(codepoint));\n9794|            }\n9795|            else if (codepoint <= 0x7ff)\n9796|            {\n9797|                // 2-byte characters: 110xxxxx 10xxxxxx\n9798|                result.append(1, static_cast<typename string_t::value_type>(0xC0 | ((codepoint >> 6) & 0x1F)));\n9799|                result.append(1, static_cast<typename string_t::value_type>(0x80 | (codepoint & 0x3F)));\n9800|            }\n9801|            else if (codepoint <= 0xffff)\n9802|            {\n9803|                // 3-byte characters: 1110xxxx 10xxxxxx 10xxxxxx\n9804|                result.append(1, static_cast<typename string_t::value_type>(0xE0 | ((codepoint >> 12) & 0x0F)));\n9805|                result.append(1, static_cast<typename string_t::value_type>(0x80 | ((codepoint >> 6) & 0x3F)));\n9806|                result.append(1, static_cast<typename string_t::value_type>(0x80 | (codepoint & 0x3F)));\n9807|            }\n9808|            else if (codepoint <= 0x10ffff)\n9809|            {\n9810|                // 4-byte characters: 11110xxx 10xxxxxx 10xxxxxx 10xxxxxx\n9811|                result.append(1, static_cast<typename string_t::value_type>(0xF0 | ((codepoint >> 18) & 0x07)));\n9812|                result.append(1, static_cast<typename string_t::value_type>(0x80 | ((codepoint >> 12) & 0x3F)));\n9813|                result.append(1, static_cast<typename string_t::value_type>(0x80 | ((codepoint >> 6) & 0x3F)));\n9814|                result.append(1, static_cast<typename string_t::value_type>(0x80 | (codepoint & 0x3F)));\n9815|            }\n9816|            else\n9817|            {\n9818|                JSON_THROW(std::out_of_range(\"code points above 0x10FFFF are invalid\"));\n9819|            }\n9820|\n9821|            return result;\n9822|        }\n9823|\n9824|        /// return name of values of type token_type (only used for errors)\n9825|        static std::string token_type_name(const token_type t)\n9826|        {\n9827|            switch (t)\n9828|            {\n9829|                case token_type::uninitialized:\n9830|                    return                  }\n10929|                }\n10930|\n10931|            }\n10932|\n10933|            return last_token_type;\n10934|        }\n10935|\n10936|        /*!\n10937|        @brief append data from the stream to the line buffer\n10938|\n10939|        This function is called by the scan() function when the end of the\n10940|        buffer (`m_limit`) is reached and the `m_cursor` pointer cannot be\n10941|        incremented without leaving the limits of the line buffer. Note re2c\n10942|        decides when to call this function.\n10943|\n10944|        If the lexer reads from contiguous storage, there is no trailing null\n10945|        byte. Therefore, this function must make sure to add these padding\n10946|        null bytes.\n10947|\n10948|        If the lexer reads from an input stream, this function reads the next\n10949|        line of the input.\n10950|\n10951|        @pre\n10952|            p p p p p p u u u u u x . . . . . .\n10953|            ^           ^       ^   ^\n10954|            m_content   m_start |   m_limit\n10955|                                m_cursor\n10956|\n10957|        @post\n10958|            u u u u u x x x x x x x . . . . . .\n10959|            ^       ^               ^\n10960|            |       m_cursor        m_limit\n10961|            m_start\n10962|            m_content\n10963|        */\n10964|        void fill_line_buffer(size_t n = 0)\n10965|        {\n10966|            // if line buffer is used, m_content points to its data\n10967|            assert(m_line_buffer.empty()\n10968|                   or m_content == reinterpret_cast<const lexer_char_t*>(m_line_buffer.data()));\n10969|\n10970|            // if line buffer is used, m_limit is set past the end of its data\n10971|            assert(m_line_buffer.empty()\n10972|                   or m_limit == m_content + m_line_buffer.size());\n10973|\n10974|            // pointer relationships\n10975|            assert(m_content <= m_start);\n10976|            assert(m_start <= m_cursor);\n10977|            assert(m_cursor <= m_limit);\n10978|            assert(m_marker == nullptr or m_marker  <= m_limit);\n10979|\n10980|            // number of processed characters (p)\n10981|            const auto num_processed_chars = static_cast<size_t>(m_start - m_content);\n10982|            // offset for m_marker wrt. to m_start\n10983|            const auto offset_marker = (m_marker == nullptr) ? 0 : m_marker - m_start;\n10984|            // number of unprocessed characters (u)\n10985|            const auto offset_cursor = m_cursor - m_start;\n10986|\n10987|            // no stream is used or end of file is reached\n10988|            if (m_stream == nullptr or m_stream->eof())\n10989|            {\n10990|                // m_start may or may not be pointing into m_line_buffer at\n10991|                // this point. We trust the standard library to do the right\n10992|                // thing. See http://stackoverflow.com/q/28142011/266378\n10993|                m_line_buffer.assign(m_start, m_limit);\n10994|\n10995|                // append n characters to make sure that there is sufficient\n10996|                // space between m_cursor and m_limit\n10997|                m_line_buffer.append(1, '\\x00');\n10998|                if (n > 0)\n10999|                {\n11000|                    m_line_buffer.append(n - 1, '\\x01');\n11001|                }\n11002|            }\n11003|            else\n11004|            {\n11005|                // delete processed characters from line buffer\n11006|                m_line_buffer.erase(0, num_processed_chars);\n11007|                // read next line from input stream\n11008|                m_line_buffer_tmp.clear();\n11009|                std::getline(*m_stream, m_line_buffer_tmp, '\\n');\n11010|\n11011|                // add line with newline symbol to the line buffer\n11012|                m_line_buffer += m_line_buffer_tmp;\n11013|                m_line_buffer.push_back('\\n');\n11014|            }\n11015|\n11016|            // set pointers\n11017|            m_content = reinterpret_cast<const lexer_char_t*>(m_line_buffer.data());\n11018|            assert(m_content != nullptr);\n11019|            m_start  = m_content;\n11020|            m_marker = m_start + offset_marker;\n11021|            m_cursor = m_start + offset_cursor;\n11022|            m_limit  = m_start + m_line_buffer.size();\n11023|        }\n11024|\n11025|        /// return string representation of last read token\n11026|        string_t get_token_string() const\n11027|        {\n11028|            assert(m_start != nullptr);\n11029|            return string_t(reinterpret_cast<typename string_t::const_pointer>(m_start),\n11030|                            static_cast<size_t>(m_cursor - m_start));\n11031|        }\n11032|\n11033|        /*!\n11034|        @brief return string value for string tokens\n11035|\n11036|        The function iterates the characters between the opening and closing\n11037|        quotes of the string value. The complete string is the range\n11038|        [m_start,m_cursor). Consequently, we iterate from m_start+1 to\n11039|        m_cursor-1.\n11040|\n11041|        We differentiate two cases:\n11042|\n11043|        1. Escaped characters. In this case, a new character is constructed\n11044|           according to the nature of the escape. Some escapes create new\n11045|           characters (e.g., `\"\\\\n\"` is replaced by `\"\\n\"`), some are copied\n11046|           as is (e.g., `\"\\\\\\\\\"`). Furthermore, Unicode escapes of the shape\n11047|           `\"\\\\uxxxx\"` need special care. In this case, to_unicode takes care\n11048|           of the construction of the values.\n11049|        2. Unescaped characters are copied as is.\n11050|\n11051|        @pre `m_cursor - m_start >= 2`, meaning the length of the last token\n11052|        is at least 2 bytes which is trivially true for any string (which\n11053|        consists of at least two quotes).\n11054|\n11055|            \" c1 c2 c3 ... \"\n11056|            ^                ^\n11057|            m_start          m_cursor\n11058|\n11059|        @complexity Linear in the length of the string.\\n\n11060|\n11061|        Lemma: The loop body will always terminate.\\n\n11062|\n11063|        Proof (by contradiction): Assume the loop body does not terminate. As\n11064|        the loop body does not contain another loop, one of the called\n11065|        functions must never return. The called functions are `std::strtoul`\n11066|        and to_unicode. Neither function can loop forever, so the loop body\n11067|        will never loop forever which contradicts the assumption that the loop\n11068|        body does not terminate, q.e.d.\\n\n11069|\n11070|        Lemma: The loop condition for the for loop is eventually false.\\n\n11071|\n11072|        Proof (by contradiction): Assume the loop does not terminate. Due to\n11073|        the above lemma, this can only be due to a tautological loop\n11074|        condition; that is, the loop condition i < m_cursor - 1 must always be\n11075|        true. Let x be the change of i for any loop iteration. Then\n11076|        m_start + 1 + x < m_cursor - 1 must hold to loop indefinitely. This\n11077|        can be rephrased to m_cursor - m_start - 2 > x. With the\n11078|        precondition, we x <= 0, meaning that the loop condition holds\n11079|        indefinitely if i is always decreased. However, observe that the value\n11080|        of i is strictly increasing with each iteration, as it is incremented\n11081|        by 1 in the iteration expression and never decremented inside the loop\n11082|        body. Hence, the loop condition will eventually be false which\n11083|        contradicts the assumption that the loop condition is a tautology,\n11084|        q.e.d.\n11085|\n11086|        @return string Similar to @ref operator[](const typename\n12371|    object_t::key_type&), `null` values are created in arrays and objects if\n12372|    necessary.\n12373|\n12374|    In particular:\n12375|    - If the JSON pointer points to an object key that does not exist, it\n12376|      is created an filled with a `null` value before a reference to it\n12377|      is returned.\n12378|    - If the JSON pointer points to an array index that does not exist, it\n12379|      is created an filled with a `null` value before a reference to it\n12380|      is returned.
